"When you ATTACK, your troops count their ATTACK; the DEFENDER counts the DEFENSE of theirs.
Counter triangle (a BONUS, not an automatic win): spearman counters knight; archer counters spearman; knight counters archer. Having the counter multiplies your WHOLE army's force by 1.5 (every troop in it, not only the countering type).

Defending is easier: defense counts x1.25 in a village and x1.5 in a castle (capital). In the open field (on a road) there is no bonus.
The side with the HIGHER effective force wins. A tie favors the DEFENDER.
The LOSER is destroyed entirely - this includes an attacker that fails against a village. The WINNER loses troops worth HALF of the loser's effective force, spread over its troop types: that amount does not depend on the winner's size, so a bigger winning army loses a smaller share of itself. A defender that holds its village loses troops the same way."

Wait, the combat rules:
"When you ATTACK, your troops count their ATTACK; the DEFENDER counts the DEFENSE of theirs."
"Counter triangle (a BONUS, not an automatic win): spearman counters knight; archer counters spearman; knight counters archer. Having the counter multiplies your force by 1.5."
"The MOST NUMEROUS type of each army sets the counter matchup (ties in count break in the order spearman, archer, knight)."
"Defending is easier: defense counts x1.25 in a village and x1.5 in a castle (capital). In the open field (on a road) there is no bonus."
"The side with the HIGHER effective force wins. A tie favors the DEFENDER."
"The winner also takes losses (attrition against the loser's effective force)."

Let's look at the defense of the neutral villages:
Valencia [16]: defense 3 (location bonus included). It has 1 spearman.
If we attack Valencia with, say, Castellon's troops: 1 spearman, 1 archer.
Our army: 1 spearman, 1 archer. Most numerous type: tie between spearman and archer. Tie breaks in order spearman, archer, knight. So spearman is most numerous (1 vs 1, tie goes to spearman).
Wait, the defender is in a village, so their defense is 3 (already includes location bonus).
Wait, the garrison is 1 spearman. The effective defense is 3.
Our attack:
Spearman: attack 1. Archer: attack 2.
Since spearman is the most numerous type (or ties), does it set the counter?
"The MOST NUMEROUS type of each army sets the counter matchup"
If our most numerous is spearman, and theirs is spearman (only 1), then spearman vs spearman (no counter bonus).
Wait, how is effective force calculated?
Usually: sum of (attack of each troop) * (counter bonus if applicable).
Let's check the exact wording:
"When you ATTACK, your troops count their ATTACK; the DEFENDER counts the DEFENSE of theirs."
"The side with the HIGHER effective force wins."
So our effective attack = sum of attack of our troops, possibly multiplied by 1.5 if we have the counter bonus.
Their effective defense = sum of defense of their troops, possibly multiplied by 1.5 if they have the counter bonus? Or does the defender also get the counter bonus?
"Counter triangle (a BONUS, not an automatic win): spearman counters knight; archer counters spearman; knight counters archer. Having the counter multiplies your force by 1.5."
Usually, the counter applies to the attacker if they counter the defender's most numerous type, or to the defender if they counter the attacker's most numerous type.
Let's assume standard rules: the side with the counter multiplies their force by 1.5.
Let's look at Valencia: garrison 1 spearman. Defense 3 (effective defense).
If we send 1 spearman, 1 archer from Castellon:
Our troops: 1 spearman (attack 1), 1 archer (attack 2). Total raw attack = 3.
Most numerous: tie, spearman wins tiebreak. So our army type is spearman.
Their army: 1 spearman (defense 2, but effective defense is 3 due to location bonus? Or is the defense 2 and location bonus makes it 3? "effective defense (location bonus included): 3". So their defense is 3).
Their most numerous type is spearman.
Our type is spearman. Spearman does not counter spearman. Archer does not counter spearman (archer counters spearman? Wait! "archer counters spearman". Ah! Archer counters spearman!)
Let's read carefully: "spearman counters knight; archer counters spearman; knight counters archer."
So archer counters spearman!
If our most numerous type is spearman, and theirs is spearman, do we get a counter? No, our most numerous is spearman, which counters knight. But their most numerous is spearman. The counter is based on the matchup between the attacker's most numerous and defender's most numerous.
If attacker's most numerous is spearman, defender's is spearman: spearman vs spearman -> no counter.
If attacker's most numerous is archer, defender's is spearman: archer counters spearman -> attacker gets 1.5x bonus!
Wait, if we have 1 spearman and 1 archer, the tiebreak says spearman is most numerous. So our type is spearman.
But what if we send only archers? Then archer is most numerous.
Let's look at the march distance:
From Castellon to Valencia: 1 slow / 1 medium / 1 fast turns.
This means if we send troops, they arrive in 1 turn (since the slowest is slow, but wait: "1 slow / 1 medium / 1 fast turns" means if we send slow troops it takes 1 turn? No, "march from [x]: 5 slow / 3 medium / 2 fast turns" means slow troops take 5 turns, medium 3, fast 2.
Here, "march from [18] Castellon: 1 slow / 1 medium / 1 fast turns" means it takes 1 turn for any speed! That's very close.
From Zaragoza to Madrid: "1 slow / 1 medium / 1 fast turns". Also 1 turn!
From Castellon to Teruel: "2 slow / 1 medium / 1 fast turns". So 2 turns if we send slow, 1 turn if fast.
From Zaragoza to Pamplona: "2 slow / 1 medium / 1 fast turns".

"Troops sent from DIFFERENT villages never add up, even when they arrive at the same target on the same turn: each send fights alone, one at a time."

Let's try to derive the loss rule from the three examples.

Example 1: Attack Badajoz. Winner: 5 spearman, 2 archers. Effective force 9. Loser: defense 8. Winner lost 3 troops. Remaining: 4 troops.
Example 2: Attack Faro. Winner: 2 archers, 1 knight. Effective force 8. Loser: defense 5. Winner lost 1 troop. Remaining: 2 troops.
Example 3: Attack Porto. Winner: 1 archer, 2 knights. Effective force 10. Loser: defense 8. Winner lost 1 troop. Remaining: 2 troops.

Let's denote:
For each attack, we have winner's effective force W, loser's effective force L (which is the defender's effective defense). Winner sends N troops. Winner loses n troops.

We want a function n = f(W, L, N) or something.

In Ex1: W=9, L=8, N=7, n=3.
Ex2: W=8, L=5, N=3, n=1.
Ex3: W=10, L=8, N=3, n=1.

Notice that in Ex2 and Ex3, N=3, but L=5 gives n=1, L=8 gives n=1. So L alone doesn't determine n. In Ex1, N=7, L=8 gives n=3. So n increases with N.

Maybe n is proportional to N * (L/W)? 
Ex1: N*L/W = 7*8/9 = 56/9 ≈ 6.22, n=3. Not half.
Ex2: 3*5/8 = 15/8 = 1.875, n=1.
Ex3: 3*8/10 = 2.4, n=1.
So n is roughly N*L/W / 2? Ex1: 6.22/2=3.11 -> 3. Ex2: 1.875/2=0.9375 -> 1. Ex3: 2.4/2=1.2 -> 1. That fits if we round? But why divide by 2? The rule says "winner loses troops worth HALF of the loser's effective force". That suggests the total "worth" of lost troops is L/2. If we think of each troop as having a "worth" equal to its contribution to the winner's effective force? In Ex1, winner's effective force 9 from 7 troops, so average worth = 9/7 ≈ 1.2857. L/2 = 4. So number of troops lost = 4 / (9/7) = 28/9 ≈ 3.11 -> 3. In Ex2: average worth = 8/3 ≈ 2.667. L/2 = 2.5. Troops lost = 2.5 / (8/3) = 7.5/8 = 0.9375 -> 1. In Ex3: average worth = 10/3 ≈ 3.333. L/2 = 4. Troops lost = 4 / (10/3) = 12/10 = 1.2 -> 1. So the formula n = round( (L/2) / (W/N) ) = round( N * L / (2W) ). That works for all three if we round to nearest integer? Ex1: 7*8/(2*9) = 56/18 = 3.111 -> 3. Ex2: 3*5/(2*8) = 15/16 = 0.9375 -> 1. Ex3: 3*8/(2*10) = 24/20 = 1.2 -> 1. So rounding gives the observed n. But what about the types of troops lost? The rule says "spread over its troop types". That likely means we lose troops of each type in proportion to their numbers in the attacking force. In Ex1, we had 5 spearman, 2 archers. Proportions: 5/7 spearman, 2/7 archer. n=3. So expected losses: 3*(5/7)=2.14 spearman, 0.86 archer. Round to 2 spearman, 1 archer. That matches our earlier deduction. In Ex2: 2 archers, 1 knight. n=1. Proportions: 2/3 archer, 1/3 knight. Expected: 0.67 archer, 0.33 knight. Round: 1 archer, 0 knight. So we lost 1 archer. In Ex3: 1 archer, 2 knights. n=1. Proportions: 1/3 archer, 2/3 knight. Expected: 0.33 archer, 0.67 knight. Round: 0 archer, 1 knight. So we lost 1 knight. That fits perfectly!

So the loss rule is: When you win an attack, you lose a number of troops equal to round( N * L / (2W) ), where N is the number of troops you sent, L is the defender's effective force (which is the effective defense value from the report, possibly modified by counter if defender gets a counter multiplier), and W is your effective force (including your counter multiplier). The troops you lose are chosen by spreading the loss proportionally across your troop types, rounding to integers (probably rounding each type's share to nearest integer, ensuring total equals n). And the remaining troops stay at the target village as garrison.

If you lose the attack, you are destroyed entirely.

Also, note that the defender's effective force L might include a counter multiplier if the defender's most numerous type counters your most numerous type. In the examples, we don't know the defender's composition, but we can assume the given defense number is the effective defense without counter, because the counter depends on the attacker's composition. So in the report, "effective defense" is the defense with location bonus but without counter. When we attack, we must compute the actual defender's effective force for combat: if defender gets a counter, their force is multiplied by 1.5. So L_actual = effective_defense * (1.5 if defender counters else 1). Similarly, our W is our base attack * (1.5 if we counter else 1).

Ah! This is a very important rule!
"Troops sent from DIFFERENT villages never add up, even when they arrive at the same target on the same turn: each send fights alone, one at a time."
This means if we send troops from Zaragoza to Madrid, and also from Tarragona to Madrid (via Zaragoza? No, Tarragona is not connected to Madrid directly, only via Zaragoza. If we send from two different villages to the same target (e.g. Zaragoza to Madrid, and some other village to Madrid), they fight separately.
But here, only Zaragoza is connected to Madrid among our villages. So we can only send from Zaragoza to Madrid.

Losses = (Defender's effective force / Attacker's effective force) * Attacker's troops, rounded somehow.

We need to consider the counter triangle. Spearman counters knight (x1.5), archer counters spearman (x1.5), knight counters archer (x1.5). So if we attack with a mixed army, the most numerous type sets the counter. If we attack Salamanca which has 3 knights (most numerous knight), then to counter we need most numerous type to be spearman. If we attack with spearmen, we get x1.5 vs knights. But Salamanca's defense is 8, which is knights. If we attack with spearmen, our attack is 1 each, but with x1.5 multiplier. If we attack with archers, they don't counter knights, so no bonus. If we attack with knights, no bonus. So to beat Salamanca, we should use spearmen.

For Toledo, Vigo, Sevilha: they have spearmen (4 each). To counter, we need archers as most numerous. So we should use archers to attack those.

But we also need to consider our own losses. The winner loses troops worth half of the loser's effective force. So we want to win with minimal losses.

Attacker: 6 spearman (attack 1), 1 archer (attack 2), 1 knight (attack 4). Total attack = 6+2+4 = 12.
Most numerous type: spearman (6). Defender most numerous: knight (3). Spearman counters knight, so attacker gets x1.5 multiplier. Effective attack = 12 * 1.5 = 18.
Defender effective defense = 8 (given).
18 > 8, attacker wins.
Losses: attacker loses troops worth half of defender's effective force = 4. The loss is spread over attacker's troop types. It says "spread over its troop types: that amount does not depend on the winner's size, so a bigger winning army loses a smaller share of itself." So we need to lose troops with total attack value equal to 4? Or defense value? The rule: "The WINNER loses troops worth HALF of the loser's effective force". Since the loser is the defender, their effective force is based on defense. The winner loses troops worth half of that. Probably it means the total attack value of the lost troops equals half the defender's effective force. Or it could be the total defense value? The rule says "that amount does not depend on the winner's size", so it's a fixed number based on the loser's force. In this case, 4. We need to lose troops whose total "worth" is 4. The worth is probably the attack value? Or maybe it's a point value? In many games, it's the attack value. Let's assume we lose troops with total attack value = 4. The rule says "spread over its troop types", meaning it is distributed proportionally?
